> AGENT

## Substance review: unsubstantiated, AI-flavored, or salesy passages

I read §1–§5 with a reviewer's skeptical eye for hype, vague claims, and language that promises more than the data delivers. **Six specific passages** stand out, ordered by severity. Each shows the offending text and the concrete fix.

---

### S1 — "minimal causal intervention" (abstract + intro + contributions, repeated 3×)

**Where:** abstract line 58, intro line 70 (implicit), contribution bullet 3 line 76.

**Problem:** "Minimal causal intervention" is sales-speak. *Causal* is loaded — it implies you ran a randomized intervention or counterfactual, but RR-MoA is an architectural change, and the eight controls (RevIN ablation, BatchNorm/GroupNorm swap, etc.) are *causal controls* on the failure mechanism, not on the fix being causal. Reviewers read "causal intervention" as overclaim. *Minimal* is also doing rhetorical work — minimal compared to what? The 853-param gate is small, but the swap from hidden-state to raw-input router is not literally a one-line change.

**Fix:** call it what it is. "RR-MoA, the targeted architectural fix that follows from the diagnosis" or just "the fix" once the mechanism is established. Keep "causal" only when describing the *controls* on the mechanism, not the proposed method.

---

### S2 — "sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone" (abstract line 58)

**Problem:** "SOTA" claims need a benchmark. You're not winning on absolute MSE — DLinear and PatchTST beat RR-MoA in Table 5 (`tab:baselines`), which the paper itself flags as the "DLinear gap." So the SOTA claim is true *only* in a narrow scope ("adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone") that excludes the methods that win on absolute MSE. A reviewer skimming the abstract reads "new SOTA," then sees DLinear ahead in Table 5 and feels misled.

**Fix:** drop "SOTA". The 54/54 wins + p<0.001 vs all baselines + cross-backbone numbers are the real claim. Replace "sets a new SOTA … " with something like "RR-MoA dominates every adapter-based baseline on the primary backbone (54/54 wins, 26–79% MSE improvement, p<0.001)…". This is what the data actually shows and is no weaker than the SOTA framing once a reviewer reads it carefully.

---

### S3 — "comprehensive baseline suite" / "comprehensive baseline comparison" (line 399, line 392)

**Problem:** "Comprehensive" is a salesman word. Seven baselines is a strong set, but it's not comprehensive — there's no NLinear, no PatchTST as a baseline (only as a calibration anchor), no recent adapter methods like AdaPTS or AdaMix variants. Reviewers will instinctively look for what's missing as soon as you say "comprehensive."

**Fix:** "a strong baseline suite" or just "a baseline suite of seven methods spanning fixed adapters, LoRA, structured adapters (TRACE), MoE adapters (AdaMix), independent ensembles, full fine-tuning, and a from-scratch DLinear anchor." Concrete > self-laudatory.

---

### S4 — "the natural mixture-of-experts approach / the natural MoE upgrade / the natural MoE remedy" (3× in §1 + abstract)

**Problem:** "Natural" is doing argumentative work that's not earned. It frames MoE as the obvious next step so that the failure of MoE feels surprising. But many reviewers will push back: "is MoE really natural here, or is this strawman framing?" The abstract uses "natural upgrade", intro ¶2 says "is what motivates a per-window mixture", intro ¶3 says "natural mixture-of-experts approach", §4.2 line 399 says "the natural MoE remedy collapses". The repetition makes it feel rhetorical.

**Fix:** keep one occurrence (the first, in §1 ¶2 where you motivate it from heterogeneous regimes). Drop "natural" from the others. The motivation is already in ¶2; you don't need the adjective to keep doing argumentative lifting.

---

### S5 — "The principle is architecture-agnostic" (line 496) + "the diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention" (line 70 implicit)

**Problem:** "Architecture-agnostic" is overclaim. You validate the principle on three architectures (RR-MoA's external Conv1d gate, SR-MoA's per-expert sigmoid, AdaMix-Raw's softmax router) — that's "across multiple router architectures we tested," not "architecture-agnostic." The latter implies a universality claim you didn't run. Vision MoE adds a fourth point but in a different modality, not a different MoE architecture.

**Fix:** "The principle generalizes across router designs" or "Three router-level controls confirm the principle is not specific to RR-MoA's gate." Keep the data, drop the universal quantifier.

---

### S6 — "powerful pretrained representations" / "the design of that adapter, more than the backbone choice, determines deployment utility" (intro line 64)

**Problem:** Two issues:
- "Powerful pretrained representations" is a content-free filler phrase. Every TSFM paper says this. It's not wrong but it's wallpaper.
- The second clause ("the design of that adapter, more than the backbone choice, determines deployment utility") is asserted with no citation or empirical support in the intro. It's the *thesis* of your paper, but stated as if already established. Reviewers reading the intro want to see this *promised* and *then proved*, not asserted as background fact.

**Fix:** rewrite line 64 to be more concrete and less self-justifying:
- Drop "powerful pretrained representations." The TSFM list does the work.
- Reframe the second clause as a question or motivation, not an assertion: "Using one of these backbones on a downstream task requires attaching a lightweight adapter head; the design of that adapter is the focus of this paper."

---

## Smaller items (mention but don't necessarily fix)

- **"healthy routing entropy ($1.0$–$1.57$)"** (line 481): "healthy" is a value judgment. Better: "non-collapsed routing entropy (1.0–1.57, where log K = 1.609 is the uniform-routing ceiling)" or just "routing entropy of 1.0–1.57 (vs. 0.000 under AdaMix)."

- **"strictly dominates"** (contribution 3, line 76): "strictly dominates" is statistically ambiguous unless every cell wins at p<0.05. You have Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon at p<0.001 vs each baseline aggregated, which is strong but is *not* the same as cell-by-cell strict dominance. Soften to "outperforms" or "beats at p<0.001."

- **"deployment utility"** (line 64): the deployment paragraph is good evidence (§B benchmark in appendix), but in the *intro* the phrase is uncashed. Either cash it (one number) or rephrase ("…determines task accuracy under the standard deployment regime").

- **"healthy" / "natural" / "comprehensive" / "powerful"** are an evaluative-adjective cluster that, taken together, gives the paper a slight self-promotional voice. NeurIPS reviewers (especially adversarial ones) downgrade for this — they want claims tied to numbers, not adjectives. The fix above for S3, S4, S6 will mostly defuse this.

---

## Recommendation

**Fix S1, S2, S3, S5 today.** They're the four "smell tests" any reviewer flags first (causal overclaim, SOTA without scope, comprehensive without enumeration, architecture-agnostic without proof). All four are pure word-swaps, no number changes, ~10 minutes.

**S4 and S6 are stylistic.** Worth doing for polish but lower priority at T-2.

**Do not touch the abstract beyond S2** — the abstract is the highest-cost edit zone for regression risk and the M1/M2 framing you have is already concrete.

58:Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with DLinear on $107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
78:\item We propose RR-MoA, the \textbf{minimal causal intervention}: changing only the router's input from normalized hidden states to $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$, with no change to the optimizer, the experts, or the frozen backbone. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against AdaMix (the same architecture with collapsed routing), strictly dominates $11$ optimization-side rescue mechanisms ($720$ runs; best rescue recovers $\leq 10.9\%$, RR-MoA $2.7{\times}$ better), and is not subsumed by naive $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection ($+42$--$239\%$ MSE; $11/18$ cells re-collapse). Two architecturally distinct variants confirm the principle generalizes beyond RR-MoA's specific router: \textbf{SR-MoA} (self-routed, no external router; outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets) and \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (expert-level raw-input dual-stream; matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets, generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones).
1225:This corollary identifies raw routing as the \emph{minimal causal fix}: it intervenes on the precise term in $\partial\ell/\partial A$ that drives the collapse loop, leaving all other components of the model untouched. The empirical validation is in Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory} (RR-MoA's gradient norms remain balanced across experts even when blocks are unfrozen) and Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} (frozen-vs-unfrozen RR-MoA both maintain healthy entropy, in contrast to AdaMix where unfreezing collapses entropy to $0.000$).
```

384:\textbf{Expert pool.} We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), spanning the canonical pooling families used in sequence representation learning~\citep{reimers2019sentencebert}. Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}). Pseudocode in Algorithm~\ref{alg:rrmoa} (Appendix~\ref{app:setup}). We now empirically validate that this minimal fix recovers routing diversity, beats the comprehensive baseline suite, and that its predictions hold under eight causal controls.
401:RR-MoA's case rests on three claims: that fixed-topology adapters are suboptimal, that the natural MoE remedy collapses on instance-normalized backbones, and that the collapse cannot be recovered by standard rescue mechanisms. We establish each in turn before comparing RR-MoA against a comprehensive baseline suite.

## Unsupported claims audit (§1–§5)

I scanned every assertive sentence in the main body for claims that should have either an in-text citation, a table/figure reference, or a numeric backstop, but currently have none. **Eight passages** flagged, ordered by severity.

---

### U1 — "the design of that adapter, more than the backbone choice, determines deployment utility" (§1 ¶1, line 64)

**Claim:** Adapter design matters more than backbone choice for deployment.
**Evidence in paper:** None. No table or experiment compares adapters across backbones holding everything else fixed in a way that isolates "adapter > backbone." The cross-backbone tables (§N) compare RR-MoA-vs-fixed *within* a backbone — they don't establish the comparative claim "more than backbone choice."
**Severity:** High. This is the *thesis-load-bearing* sentence of the intro and it's an unsupported comparative claim. A skeptical reviewer will challenge it directly.
**Fix:** Soften or rephrase. "the design of that adapter is the focus of this paper" or "adapter design is a leading determinant of deployment utility (§\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, App.~\ref{app:benchmark})". Or cite a paper that has shown this.

---

### U2 — "spanning the canonical pooling families used in sequence representation learning" (§3, line 382)

**Claim:** mean/last/max/attention/Conv1d "span the canonical pooling families."
**Evidence in paper:** Cites `reimers2019sentencebert`, but that paper covers mean/max/CLS pooling for *sentence embeddings*, not "sequence representation learning" as a whole, and doesn't use Conv1d-pool. The citation does not actually support "canonical families."
**Severity:** Medium. Reviewer will note the citation doesn't carry the claim's weight.
**Fix:** Either drop "canonical" ("…spanning five pooling families: mean, last-token, max, attention, Conv1d.") or add a stronger citation that actually surveys pooling choices.

---

### U3 — "Architectural diversity is critical" (§3, line 382)

**Claim:** "Architectural diversity is critical." This is a generalization above what the data shows.
**Evidence in paper:** The 8–15% MSE degradation on identical experts (App.~\ref{app:diversity}) supports the *narrower* claim "topological diversity helps in our setup."
**Severity:** Low–medium. Word choice issue more than substance. "Critical" is stronger than the 8–15% effect warrants.
**Fix:** "Architectural diversity matters" or "Topological diversity in the expert pool yields measurable gains: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by 8–15% (App.~\ref{app:diversity})."

---

### U4 — "RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes" (§1 ¶3, line 68)

**Claim:** RevIN strips μ and σ and the router *needs* them. The first part has the citation; the second part ("the router needs them") is the unsupported half.
**Evidence in paper:** Comes later (Observation 1, signal-ratio diagnostic, dose-response). At the point of utterance in §1, the reader has only the assertion.
**Severity:** Medium. It's the load-bearing mechanistic claim of the paper, asserted before evidence.
**Fix:** Add forward pointer: "…that the router needs to distinguish input regimes (formalized in Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}; signal-ratio diagnostic Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio})." This converts "trust me" into "we'll prove this in §X."

---

### U5 — "what motivates a per-window mixture of expert adapters" (§1 ¶2, line 66)

**Claim:** "Residual *shape* heterogeneity (attention patterns, temporal structure) persists in the hidden states." Asserted as fact.
**Evidence in paper:** Not directly demonstrated in the main body. The diagnostic appendix (§M / `tab:diagnostic`) shows information-content diagnostics but the specific "residual shape heterogeneity persists in hidden states" claim is asserted, not proven.
**Severity:** Medium. The claim is plausible but the paper doesn't independently demonstrate it; it functions as motivation.
**Fix:** Cushion as motivation, not fact. "Residual *shape* heterogeneity (attention patterns, temporal structure) is plausibly retained in the hidden states, motivating per-window expert routing." Or add a citation if any prior work has shown this.

---

### U6 — "complementary rather than competing" (§2 Related Work, line 230)

**Claim:** RR-MoA is *complementary* to in-backbone MoE (Moirai-MoE, Time-MoE).
**Evidence in paper:** Backed by `tab:moirai_moe` (one experiment showing RR-MoA on top of Moirai-MoE improves accuracy). The wording "complementary rather than competing" is broader than one experiment supports.
**Severity:** Low. The cite/forward-pointer is there (`§\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}`), so this is more about hedge wording than missing evidence.
**Fix:** Optional. "stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy on the datasets we tested (§\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe})". Drops "complementary rather than competing" as an opinion.

---

### U7 — "exactly as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts" (§4.2, line 481)

**Claim:** Traffic's no-improvement is "exactly" what Observation 1 predicts.
**Evidence in paper:** Observation 1 (line 555+) defines $R(\mathcal{D})$ and the signal-ratio. The prediction is *monotone*: low R → little benefit. Traffic R=0.14 having Δ=+2.9% is consistent with that monotone prediction but "exactly" overstates the precision (Observation 1 doesn't predict a specific MSE delta).
**Severity:** Low. Word choice. "Exactly" is precise-sounding but the prediction is qualitative.
**Fix:** "…consistent with Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}'s prediction" or "…as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (low $R$ → no benefit)."

---

### U8 — "AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix}, which uses the standard recipe of routing on hidden states" (§4.2, line 461)

**Claim:** AdaMix's "standard recipe" is "routing on hidden states." This is a representation of someone else's method.
**Evidence in paper:** AdaMix's actual paper proposes random routing during training and averaging during inference; describing it as "standard recipe of routing on hidden states" is a simplification but defensible — your `run_adamix.py` uses softmax-on-hidden as the AdaMix-style baseline, and the appendix `app:adamix_details` documents this. But the in-text representation is one-sided.
**Severity:** Low–medium. Reviewer familiar with AdaMix may flag "but the original AdaMix uses random routing, not learned softmax."
**Fix:** Either (a) drop the AdaMix label and call it "the standard hidden-state MoE recipe (load balancing on hidden-state router)," or (b) cite the AdaMix appendix where you document your specific implementation: "AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} adapted to a learned hidden-state router (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details})."

---

## Smaller items

- **"deployment utility"** in §1 ¶1 (line 64) is asserted with `(Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark})`. The forward pointer is good — but the *intro* sentence reads as established fact even without flipping to the appendix. Consider whether to surface one number ("…hot-swapped from host RAM in <X seconds, App.~\ref{app:benchmark}").

- **"the natural mixture-of-experts approach fails"** (§1 ¶3, line 68) is supported by Table 2 forward pointer — adequate.

- **"because the problem lies in the router's *input*, not its optimization"** (§1 ¶3, line 70 in new layout) is supported by Table 2 + rescue sweep forward pointer — adequate.

- **"Standard TSFM adapters … pool hidden states and project them"** (§1 ¶2, line 66) cites four papers — adequate.

---

## Recommendation

**Top priority** (load-bearing intro claims that reviewers will challenge):
- **U1** (adapter > backbone): rewrite or soften.
- **U4** (RevIN strips routing signal): add forward pointer to Observation 1.

**Medium** (fixable in one minute each):
- **U2** ("canonical" pooling families): drop "canonical" or strengthen citation.
- **U5** (residual shape heterogeneity): cushion as motivation.
- **U8** (AdaMix description): add appendix forward pointer.

**Low** (stylistic):
- **U3** ("critical"), **U6** ("complementary rather than competing"), **U7** ("exactly").

Want me to execute U1 + U4 first (the two highest-stakes ones), then proceed?

|---|---|---|
| **U1** | "adapter design > backbone choice for deployment utility" | **Search**: efficient-PEFT/adapter literature for TSFMs or LLMs that argues parameter-efficient adaptation drives deployment economics more than backbone-scaling. Hanafy1 Mani Srivastava2† Prashant Shenoy1 1University of Massachusetts Amherst 2University of California Los Angeles\n\n###### Abstract\n\nFoundation models (FMs) have opened new avenues for machine learning applications due to their ability to adapt to new and unseen tasks with minimal or no further training. Time-series foundation models (TSFMs)—FMs trained on time-series data—have shown strong performance on classification, regression, and imputation tasks. Recent pipelines combine TSFMs with task-specific encoders, decoders, and adapters to improve performance; however, assembling such pipelines typically requires ad hoc, model-specific implementations that hinder modularity and reproducibility. We introduce FMTK, an open-source, lightweight and extensible toolkit for constructing and fine-tuning TSFM pipelines via standardized backbone and component abstractions. FMTK enables flexible composition across models and tasks, achieving correctness and performance with an average of seven lines of code. https://github.com/umassos/FMTK\n\n††footnotetext: Author holds concurrent appointments as a Professor at UCLA, and as an Amazon Scholar. While these models serve as fixed backbones trained on large-scale temporal data, effective task specialization often requires integrating additional components: input encoders to structure raw data, task-specific decoders to generate predictions, and increasingly, parameter-efficient adapters (e.g., LoRA Hu2022:LoRA) to enable lightweight fine-tuning.\n\nThis modular design space, though conceptually flexible, has resulted in a fragmented and ad hoc implementation landscape. For example, models such as PaPaGei pillai2024papagei and Mantis feofanov2025mantis require the development of extensive custom pipelines for simple comparison with state-of-the-art models. Moreover, models like Moment Goswami2024:MOMENT offer distinct modes for different tasks (e.g., forecasting versus classification), which restricts the reuse of the same powerful backbone for a diverse set of downstream tasks during runtime. As a result, three key challenges emerge. 1 First, the absence of a unifying abstraction across encoders, backbones, adapters, and decoders significantly increases the engineering burden and inhibits systematic exploration of architectural variants. 2 Second, the lack of modular encapsulation complicates the attribution.       "text": "[2602.11550] TS-Memory: Plug-and-Play Memory for Time Series Foundation Models\n\n -->\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2602.11550 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 12 Feb 2026]\n\n# Title:TS-Memory: Plug-and-Play Memory for Time Series Foundation Models\n\nAuthors: Sisuo Lyu, Siru Zhong, Tiegang Chen, Weilin Ruan, Qingxiang Liu, Taiqiang Lv, Qingsong Wen, Raymond Chi-Wing Wong, Yuxuan Liang\n\nView PDF HTML (experimental)\n\n> Abstract:Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) achieve strong zero-shot forecasting through large-scale pre-training, but adapting them to downstream domains under distribution shift remains challenging. Existing solutions face a trade-off: Parametric Adaptation can cause catastrophic forgetting and requires costly multi-domain maintenance, while Non-Parametric Retrieval improves forecasts but incurs high inference latency due to datastore search. We propose Parametric Memory Distillation and implement it as TS-Memory, a lightweight memory adapter that augments frozen TSFMs. TS-Memory is trained in two stages. First, we construct an offline, leakage-safe kNN teacher that synthesizes confidence-aware quantile targets from retrieved futures. Second, we distill this retrieval-induced distributional correction into a lightweight memory adapter via confidence-gated supervision. During inference, TS-Memory fuses memory and backbone predictions with constant-time overhead, enabling retrieval-free deployment. Experiments across diverse TSFMs and benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements in both point and probabilistic forecasting over representative adaptation methods, with efficiency comparable to the frozen backbone.\n\n \n\nhttps://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2602.11550\n\n \n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI) |\n| --- | --- |\n| Cite as: | arXiv:2602.11550 [cs.LG] |\n| (or arXiv:2602.11550v1 [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Sisuo Lyu [view email] [v1] Thu, 12 Feb 2026 04:16:19 UTC (9,124 KB)\n\n \n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\n- View PDF\n- HTML (experimental)\n- TeX Source\n\nview license\n\n \n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n< prev| next >\n\nnew| recent| 2026-02\n\nChange to browse by:\n\ncs cs.AI\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- Google Scholar\n- Semantic Scholar\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\n \n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliographic Explorer (What is the Explorer?)\n\nConnected Papers Toggle\n\nConnected Papers (What is Connected Papers?)\n\nLitmaps Toggle\n\nLitmaps (What is Litmaps?)\n\nscite.ai Toggle\n\nscite Smart Citations (What are Smart Citations?)\n\nCode, Data, Media\n\n# Code, Data and Media Associated with this Article\n\nalphaXiv Toggle\n\nalphaXiv (What is alphaXiv?)\n\nLinks to Code Toggle\n\nCatalyzeX Code Finder for Papers (What is CatalyzeX?)\n\nDagsHub Toggle\n\nDagsHub (What is DagsHub?)\n\nGotitPub Toggle\n\nGotit.pub (What is GotitPub?)\n\nHuggingface Toggle\n\nHugging Face (What is Huggingface?)\n\nLinks ",

      "text": "MEMTS: Internalizing Domain Knowledge via Parameterized\nMemory for Retrieval-Free Domain Adaptation of Time Series\nFoundation Models\nXiaoyun Yu∗\nEast China Normal\nUniversity\nShanghai, China\nxyyu@stu.ecnu.edu.cn\nLi Fan∗\nEast China Normal\nUniversity\nShanghai, China\nlfan@stu.ecnu.edu.cn\nXiangfei Qiu\nEast China Normal\nUniversity\nShanghai, China\nxfqiu@stu.ecnu.edu.cn\nNanqing Dong\nShanghai Artificial\nIntelligence Laboratory\nShanghai, China\ndongnanqing@pjlab.org.cn\nYonggui Huang\nPeking University\nBeijing, China\nyghuang@pku.edu.cn\nHonggang Qi\nUniversity of Chinese\nAcademy of Sciences\nBeijing, China\nhgqi@ucas.ac.cn\nGeguang Pu\nEast China Normal\nUniversity\nShanghai, China\nggpu@sei.ecnu.edu.cn\nWanli Ouyang\nShanghai Artificial\nIntelligence Laboratory\nShanghai, China\nouyangwanli@pjlab.org.cn\nXi Chen†\nEast China Normal\nUniversity\nShanghai, China\nxchen@geo.ecnu.edu.cn\nJilin Hu†\nEast China Normal\nUniversity\nShanghai, China\njlhu@dase.ecnu.edu.cn\nAbstract\nWhile Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) have demonstrated\nexceptional performance in generalized forecasting, their perfor\u0002mance often degrades significantly when deployed in real-world\nvertical domains characterized by temporal distribution shifts and\ndomain-specific periodic structures. Current solutions are primar\u0002ily constrained by two paradigms: Domain-Adaptive Pretraining\n(DAPT), which improves short-term domain fitting but frequently\ndisrupts previously learned global temporal patterns due to cata\u0002strophic forgetting; and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG),\nwhich incorporates external knowledge but introduces substan\u0002tial retrieval overhead. This creates a severe scalability bottleneck\nthat fails to meet the high-efficiency requirements of real-time\nstream processing. To break this impasse, we propose MEMory for\nTime Series (MEMTS), a lightweight and plug-and-play method for\nretrieval-free domain adaptation in time series forecasting. The key\ncomponent of MEMTS is a Knowledge Persistence Module (KPM),\nwhich internalizes domain-specific temporal dynamics, such as re\u0002curring seasonal patterns and trends into a compact set of learnable\nlatent prototypes. In doing so, it transforms fragmented historical\nobservations into continuous, parameterized knowledge represen\u0002tations. This paradigm shift enables MEMTS to achieve accurate\ndomain adaptation with constant-time inference and near-zero la\u0002tency, while effectively mitigating catastrophic forgetting of general\ntemporal patterns, all without requiring any architectural modifi\u0002cations to the frozen TSFM backbone. Extensive experiments on\nmultiple datasets demonstrate the SOTA performance of MEMTS.\n∗Both authors contributed equally to this research.\n†Corresponding authors.\nCCS Concepts\n• Computing methodologies → Transfer learning; Neural net\u0002works; • Applied computing → Forecasting.\nKeywords\nTime Series Foundation Models, Domain Adaptation\n1 Introduction\nTime series forecasting leverages historical data to predict future\nvalues [28, 35, 36, 40, 52], serving "

      "title": "Compress then Serve: Serving Thousands of LoRA Adapters with Little Overhead",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2407.00066v3",
      "text": "Compress then Serve: Serving Thousands of LoRA Adapters with Little Overhead\n\n# Compress then Serve: Serving Thousands of LoRA Adapters with Little Overhead\n\nRickard Brüel Gabrielsson Jiacheng Zhu Onkar Bhardwaj Leshem Choshen Kristjan Greenewald Mikhail Yurochkin Justin Solomon\n\n###### Abstract\n\nFine-tuning large language models (LLMs) with low-rank adaptations (LoRAs) has become common practice, often yielding numerous copies of the same LLM differing only in their LoRA updates. This paradigm presents challenges for systems that serve real-time responses to queries that each involve a different LoRA. Prior works optimize the design of such systems but still require continuous loading and offloading of LoRAs, as it is infeasible to store thousands of LoRAs in GPU memory. To mitigate this issue, we investigate the efficacy of compression when serving LoRAs. We propose a method for the joint compression of LoRAs into a shared basis paired with LoRA-specific scaling matrices. We extend our algorithm to learn clusters of LoRAs that are amenable to joint compression, allowing it to scale gracefully to large LoRA collections. Our experiments with up to 1000 LoRAs111 https://huggingface.co/Lots-of-LoRAs demonstrate that compressed LoRAs preserve performance while offering major throughput gains in realistic serving scenarios with over a thousand LoRAs, maintaining 80% of the throughput of serving a single LoRA.\n\nMachine Learning, ICML\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nThe myriad uses for foundation models (FMs) have led to a proliferation of specialized models, each fine-tuned to perform a downstream task. To avoid fine-tuning foundation models with billions of parameters, parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) algorithms were proposed. An especially successful PEFT method is low-rank adaptation (LoRA) (Hu et al., 2021), which learns low-rank additive changes to neural network matrices. Because of the low-rank parameterization, these matrices (called adapter weights) contain orders of magnitude fewer parameters than the base model. Still, LoRA can achieve performance on par with full fine-tuning (Hu et al., 2021).\n\nFigure 1: Throughput gains when serving 1000s of compressed LoRAs with vLLM.\n\nLoRA’s popularity has triggered a growing need to serve large collections of LoRA adapters at scale. Proprietary and open-source LLM providers offer fine-tuning services (OpenAI, 2024; TogetherAI, 2024; Predibase, 2024) with user bases likely in the thousands or even hundreds of thousands. As each user wants to use their own fine-tuned version of the LLM, serving a dedicated fine-tuned LLM per user becomes infeasible. To this end, S-LoRA (Sheng et al., 2023) proposes a system where only the base LLM is placed on an inference server and individual LoRA adapters are switched as needed at inference time. S-LoRA optimizes the system’s inner workings via custom CUDA kernels and memory management to increase throughput when serving multiple LoRAs. Multi-LoRA system design has also bee",

However, the traditional centralized\nlearning methods may no longer be feasible due to privacy\nconcerns, computational burden, and communication over\u0002head [23].       "id": "https://tsfm.ai/blog/lora-peft-time-series-foundation-models",
      "title": "LoRA and PEFT for Time Series Foundation Models: A Technical Guide — TSFM.ai",
      "url": "https://tsfm.ai/blog/lora-peft-time-series-foundation-models",
      "publishedDate": "2026-02-23T18:24:57.000Z",
      "text": "LoRA and PEFT for Time Series Foundation Models: A Technical Guide — TSFM.ai\n\n---\n\nZero-shot inference is the default way most practitioners use time series foundation models. Hand the model a history window, get a forecast back, no training required. It works remarkably well — well enough that zero-shot TSFMs now dominate leaderboards like FEV Bench and GIFT-Eval. But zero-shot has a ceiling. When your data distribution diverges significantly from the pretraining corpus — clinical vital signs, proprietary sensor telemetry, niche financial instruments — that ceiling becomes visible fast.\n\nThe traditional answer is full fine-tuning: update every parameter on your domain-specific data. This works, but it is expensive, prone to overfitting on small datasets, and risks catastrophic forgetting of the general knowledge that made the foundation model useful in the first place. Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) offers a middle path: freeze the pretrained weights, inject small trainable low-rank matrices into specific layers, and adapt the model with a fraction of the parameters. The technique has transformed LLM fine-tuning since Hu et al. introduced it in 2021. Its application to TSFMs is newer, less standardized, and — as we will see — architecturally dependent in ways that matter.\n\nThis post covers the technical details: which TSFM architectures support LoRA, which layers to target, what rank values to use, how tokenization interacts with adaptation, what the research shows about practical improvements, and where the failure modes hide. We link to all models we host throughout so you can connect theory to available inference endpoints.\n\n## #The LoRA Mechanism, Briefly\n\nFor readers familiar with the technique, skip ahead. For those who want the mechanics:\n\nA pretrained weight matrix W₀ ∈ ℝ^(d×k) is frozen. Two small matrices A ∈ ℝ^(d×r) and B ∈ ℝ^(r×k) are added, where r << min(d, k). During the forward pass, the output becomes:\n\n```\nh = W₀x + BAx\n\n```\n\nThe scaling factor α/r controls the magnitude of the adaptation, where α (lora_alpha) is a hyperparameter. Only A and B are trained. For a layer with d=768 and k=768, full fine-tuning trains 589,824 parameters. With r=8, LoRA trains only 12,288 — a 98% reduction.\n\nAt inference time, the LoRA matrices can be merged into the base weights (W = W₀ + BA), adding zero latency overhead. This is a critical property for production deployment: you get the accuracy benefits of adaptation with identical inference cost.\n\n## #TSFM Architecture Taxonomy for LoRA\n\nHow LoRA applies to a TSFM depends fundamentally on its architecture. The current landscape has three dominant families, each with different adaptation characteristics.\n\n### #Encoder-Only Models\n\nExamples we host: Chronos-2(120M), MOMENT(385M), Moirai 1.1-R(14M-311M)\n\nEncoder-only TSFMs process the full input sequence through self-attention layers and project the final representations through a task-specific head (forecasting, anomaly detection, classification, impu",

      "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.00073",
      "text": "Test-Time Adaptation for Non-stationary Time Series:\nFrom Synthetic Regime Shifts to Financial Markets\nYurui Wu, Qingying Deng, Wonou Chung, Mairui Li\nDepartment of Statistics & Operations Research, UNC Chapel Hill\nChapel Hill, NC, 27599\n{yuruiwu,doradeng,wchung,mairuili}@email.unc.edu\nAbstract\nTime series encountered in practice are rarely stationary. When the data distribution\nchanges, a forecasting model trained on past observations can lose accuracy. We\nstudy a small-footprint test-time adaptation (TTA) framework for causal time\u0002series forecasting and direction classification. The backbone is frozen, and only\nnormalization affine parameters are updated using recent unlabeled windows. For\nclassification we minimize entropy and enforce temporal consistency; for regression\nwe minimize prediction variance across weak time-preserving augmentations and\noptionally distill from an EMA teacher. A quadratic drift penalty and an uncertainty\u0002triggered fallback keep updates stable. We evaluate this framework in two stages:\nsynthetic regime shifts on ETT benchmarks, and daily equity and FX series (SPY,\nQQQ, EUR/USD) across pandemic, high-inflation, and recovery regimes. On\nsynthetic gradual drift, normalization-based TTA improves forecasting error, while\nin financial markets a simple batch-normalization statistics update is a robust\ndefault and more aggressive norm-only adaptation can even hurt. Our results\nprovide practical guidance for deploying TTA on non-stationary time series. The\nsource code of this study is available at this Google Drive.\n1 Introduction\nReal-world time series, such as financial prices or electricity loads, evolve over time. Structural\nbreaks, policy changes, and macroeconomic shocks can shift both the level and the volatility of the\nprocess. During the COVID-19 crisis and the subsequent high-inflation period, market dynamics\nchanged rapidly, and models trained on pre-crisis data became miscalibrated. Once the deployment\ndistribution departs from the training distribution, minimizing training loss is no longer sufficient to\nguarantee test performance.\nTest-time adaptation (TTA) addresses this issue by updating part of the model using unlabeled test\ninputs. For time series, a practical TTA scheme must be causal (no future data), parameter efficient,\nand robust to noisy updates. In this work we freeze the backbone and adapt only a small parameter set\nat deployment. When day t arrives, we collect the most recent windows, apply weak time-preserving\naugmentations, and take a few gradient steps on unsupervised objectives. For classification we\ncombine entropy minimization with temporal consistency; for regression we minimize augmentation\nvariance and optionally distill from an exponential-moving-average teacher. We control the size of\ndaily parameter changes and fall back to a lightweight batch-normalization statistics update when an\nuncertainty proxy is high.\nOur goal is not to propose a new heavy architecture, but to understand when smal"

      "title": "Why and when should you pool? Analyzing Pooling in Recurrent Architectures - ACL Anthology",
      "url": "https://aclanthology.org/2020.findings-emnlp.410/",
      "text": "Why and when should you pool? Analyzing Pooling in Recurrent Architectures - ACL Anthology\n\nPratyush Maini, Keshav Kolluru, Danish Pruthi, Mausam\n\n---\n\n##### Abstract\n\nPooling-based recurrent neural architectures consistently outperform their counterparts without pooling on sequence classification tasks. However, the reasons for their enhanced performance are largely unexamined. In this work, we examine three commonly used pooling techniques (mean-pooling, max-pooling, and attention, and propose *max-attention*, a novel variant that captures interactions among predictive tokens in a sentence. Using novel experiments, we demonstrate that pooling architectures substantially differ from their non-pooling equivalents in their learning ability and positional biases: (i) pooling facilitates better gradient flow than BiLSTMs in initial training epochs, and (ii) BiLSTMs are biased towards tokens at the beginning and end of the input, whereas pooling alleviates this bias. Consequently, we find that pooling yields large gains in low resource scenarios, and instances when salient words lie towards the middle of the input. Across several text classification tasks, we find max-attention to frequently outperform other pooling techniques.\n\nAnthology ID:2020.findings-emnlp.410Volume: Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: EMNLP 2020 Month:NovemberYear:2020Address:OnlineEditors: Trevor Cohn, Yulan He, Yang Liu Venue: Findings SIG:Publisher:Association for Computational LinguisticsNote:Pages:4568–4586Language:URL: https://aclanthology.org/2020.findings-emnlp.410/ DOI: 10.18653/v1/2020.findings-emnlp.410 Bibkey:maini-etal-2020-poolCite (ACL):Pratyush Maini, Keshav Kolluru, Danish Pruthi, and Mausam. 2020. Why and when should you pool? Analyzing Pooling in Recurrent Architectures. In Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: EMNLP 2020, pages 4568–4586, Online. Association for Computational Linguistics.Cite (Informal): Why and when should you pool?       "text": "Enhancing Sentence Embedding with Generalized Pooling\nQian Chen\nUniversity of Science and\nTechnology of China\ncq1231@mail.ustc.edu.cn\nZhen-Hua Ling\nUniversity of Science and\nTechnology of China\nzhling@ustc.edu.cn\nXiaodan Zhu\nECE, Queen’s University\nxiaodan.zhu@queensu.ca\nAbstract\nPooling is an essential component of a wide variety of sentence representation and embedding\nmodels. This paper explores generalized pooling methods to enhance sentence embedding. We\npropose vector-based multi-head attention that includes the widely used max pooling, mean pool\u0002ing, and scalar self-attention as special cases. The model benefits from properly designed penal\u0002ization terms to reduce redundancy in multi-head attention. We evaluate the proposed model\non three different tasks: natural language inference (NLI), author profiling, and sentiment clas\u0002sification. The experiments show that the proposed model achieves significant improvement\nover strong sentence-encoding-based methods, resulting in state-of-the-art performances on four\ndatasets. The proposed approach can be easily implemented for more problems than we discuss\nin this paper.\n1 Introduction\nDistributed representation learned with neural networks has shown to be effective in modeling natural\nlanguage at different granularities. Learning representation for words (Bengio et al., 2000; Mikolov et\nal., 2013; Pennington et al., 2014), for example, has achieved notable success. Much remains to be done\nto model larger spans of text such as sentences or documents. The approaches to computing sentence\nembedding generally fall into two categories. The first consists of learning sentence embedding with\nunsupervised learning, e.g., auto-encoder-based models (Socher et al., 2011), Paragraph Vector (Le and\nMikolov, 2014), SkipThought vectors (Kiros et al., 2015), FastSent (Hill et al., 2016), among others.\nThe second category consists of models trained with supervised learning, such as convolution neural\nnetworks (CNN) (Kim, 2014; Kalchbrenner et al., 2014), recurrent neural networks (RNN) (Conneau et\nal., 2017; Bowman et al., 2015), and tree-structure recursive networks (Socher et al., 2013; Zhu et al.,\n2015; Tai et al., 2015), just to name a few.\nPooling is an essential component of a wide variety of sentence representation and embedding models.\nFor example, in recurrent-neural-network-based models, pooling is often used to aggregate hidden states\nat different time steps (i.e., words in a sentence) to obtain sentence embedding. Convolutional neural\nnetworks (CNN) also often uses max or mean pooling to obtain a fixed-size sentence embedding.\nIn this paper we explore generalized pooling methods to enhance sentence embedding. Specifically,\nby extending scalar self-attention models such as those proposed in Lin et al. (2017), we propose vector\u0002based multi-head attention, which includes the widely used max pooling, mean pooling, and scalar self\u0002attention itself as special cases.       "text": "Baseline Needs More Love: On Simple Word-Embedding-Based Models\nand Associated Pooling Mechanisms\nDinghan Shen1, Guoyin Wang1, Wenlin Wang1, Martin Renqiang Min2\nQinliang Su3, Yizhe Zhang4, Chunyuan Li1, Ricardo Henao1, Lawrence Carin1\n1 Duke University 2 NEC Laboratories America 3 Sun Yat-sen University 4 Microsoft Research\ndinghan.shen@duke.edu\nAbstract\nMany deep learning architectures have\nbeen proposed to model the composition\u0002ality in text sequences, requiring a sub\u0002stantial number of parameters and ex\u0002pensive computations. However, there\nhas not been a rigorous evaluation re\u0002garding the added value of sophisticated\ncompositional functions. In this paper,\nwe conduct a point-by-point comparative\nstudy between Simple Word-Embedding\u0002based Models (SWEMs), consisting of\nparameter-free pooling operations, rela\u0002tive to word-embedding-based RNN/CNN\nmodels. Surprisingly, SWEMs exhibit\ncomparable or even superior performance\nin the majority of cases considered. Based\nupon this understanding, we propose two\nadditional pooling strategies over learned\nword embeddings: (i) a max-pooling\noperation for improved interpretability;\nand (ii) a hierarchical pooling operation,\nwhich preserves spatial (n-gram) informa\u0002tion within text sequences. We present\nexperiments on 17 datasets encompassing\nthree tasks: (i) (long) document classifi\u0002cation; (ii) text sequence matching; and\n(iii) short text tasks, including classifica\u0002tion and tagging. The source code and\ndatasets can be obtained from https://\ngithub.com/dinghanshen/SWEM.\n1 Introduction\nWord embeddings, learned from massive unstruc\u0002tured text data, are widely-adopted building blocks\nfor Natural Language Processing (NLP). By rep\u0002resenting each word as a fixed-length vector,\nthese embeddings can group semantically simi\u0002lar words, while implicitly encoding rich linguis\u0002tic regularities and patterns (Bengio et al., 2003;\nMikolov et al., 2013; Pennington et al., 2014).\nLeveraging the word-embedding construct, many\ndeep architectures have been proposed to model\nthe compositionality in variable-length text se\u0002quences. These methods range from simple op\u0002erations like addition (Mitchell and Lapata, 2010;\nIyyer et al., 2015), to more sophisticated compo\u0002sitional functions such as Recurrent Neural Net\u0002works (RNNs) (Tai et al., 2015; Sutskever et al.,\n2014), Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs)\n(Kalchbrenner et al., 2014; Kim, 2014; Zhang\net al., 2017a) and Recursive Neural Networks\n(Socher et al., 2011a).\nModels with more expressive compositional\nfunctions, e.g., RNNs or CNNs, have demon\u0002strated impressive results; however, they are typ\u0002ically computationally expensive, due to the need\nto estimate hundreds of thousands, if not millions,\nof parameters (Parikh et al., 2016). In contrast,\nmodels with simple compositional functions often\ncompute a sentence or document embedding by\nsimply adding, or averaging, over the word em\u0002bedding of each sequence element obtained via,\ne.g., word2vec (Mikolov et al., 2013), or GloVe\n(Penni"

      "title": "[2510.03339] Pool Me Wisely: On the Effect of Pooling in Transformer-Based Models",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.03339",
      "author": "Sofiane Ennadir; Levente Zólyomi; Oleg Smirnov; Tianze Wang; John Pertoft; Filip Cornell; Lele Cao",
      "text": "[2510.03339] Pool Me Wisely: On the Effect of Pooling in Transformer-Based Models\n\n# Pool Me Wisely: On the Effect of Pooling in Transformer-Based Models\n\nSofiane Ennadir∗ King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming sofiane.ennadir@king.com &Levente Zólyomi∗† NXAI GmbH levente.zolyomi@nx-ai.com &Oleg Smirnov King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming oleg.smirnov@microsoft.com &Tianze Wang† Kreditz AB tianze.wang@kreditz.com &John Pertoft King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming john.pertoft@king.com &Filip Cornell† Amazon filipco@amazon.com &Lele Cao King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming lelecao@microsoft.com\n\n###### Abstract\n\nTransformer models have become the dominant backbone for sequence modeling, leveraging self-attention to produce contextualized token representations. These are typically aggregated into fixed-size vectors via pooling operations for downstream tasks. While much of the literature has focused on attention mechanisms, the role of pooling remains underexplored despite its critical impact on model behavior. In this paper, we introduce a theoretical framework that rigorously characterizes the expressivity of Transformer-based models equipped with widely used pooling methods by deriving closed-form bounds on their representational capacity and the ability to distinguish similar inputs. Our analysis extends to different variations of attention formulations, demonstrating that these bounds hold across diverse architectural variants. We empirically evaluate pooling strategies across tasks requiring both global and local contextual understanding, spanning three major modalities: computer vision, natural language processing, and time-series analysis. Results reveal consistent trends in how pooling choices affect accuracy, sensitivity, and optimization behavior. Our findings unify theoretical and empirical perspectives, providing practical guidance for selecting or designing pooling mechanisms suited to specific tasks. This work positions pooling as a key architectural component in Transformer models and lays the foundation for more principled model design beyond attention alone.\n\n**footnotetext: Equal contribution.$\\dagger$$\\dagger$footnotetext: Work conducted while at King AI Labs.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nThe profound impact of the Transformer (vaswani2017attention, ) architectures across different modalities and in cross-modal modeling cannot be underestimated. These models have become the building stones of large-scale systems capable of successfully addressing a multitude of downstream tasks in computer vision (awais2025foundation, ), natural language processing (NLP) (touvron2023llama, ; jiang2023mistral7b, ), and time series analysis (liang2024foundation, ; goswami2024moment, ). Since these models typically require substantial training data to perform effectively, pre-trained large-scale models trained with self-supervised objectives such as autoregressive, autoencoding, contrastive, or hybrid formulations have become the standard. These models are first trained to capture ",

      "text": "Enhancing Sentence Embedding with Generalized Pooling - ACL Anthology\n\nQian Chen, Zhen-Hua Ling, Xiaodan Zhu\n\n---\n\n##### Abstract\n\nPooling is an essential component of a wide variety of sentence representation and embedding models. This paper explores generalized pooling methods to enhance sentence embedding. We propose vector-based multi-head attention that includes the widely used max pooling, mean pooling, and scalar self-attention as special cases. The model benefits from properly designed penalization terms to reduce redundancy in multi-head attention. We evaluate the proposed model on three different tasks: natural language inference (NLI), author profiling, and sentiment classification. The experiments show that the proposed model achieves significant improvement over strong sentence-encoding-based methods, resulting in state-of-the-art performances on four datasets. The proposed approach can be easily implemented for more problems than we discuss in this paper.\n\nAnthology ID:C18-1154Volume: Proceedings of the 27th International Conference on Computational Linguistics Month:AugustYear:2018Address:Santa Fe, New Mexico, USAEditors: Emily M.       "text": "Comparative Analysis of Pooling Mechanisms in LLMs: A\nSentiment Analysis Perspective\nJinming Xing\nNorth Carolina State University\njxing6@ncsu.edu\nDongwen Luo\nSouth China University of Technology\n976267567ldw@gmail.com\nChang Xue\nYeshiva University\ncxue@mail.yu.edu\nRuilin Xing\nGuangxi University\nruilinxing8@gmail.com\nABSTRACT\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized natural lan\u0002guage processing (NLP) by delivering state-of-the-art performance\nacross a variety of tasks. Among these, Transformer-based models\nlike BERT and GPT rely on pooling layers to aggregate token-level\nembeddings into sentence-level representations. Common pool\u0002ing mechanisms such as Mean, Max, and Weighted Sum play a\npivotal role in this aggregation process. Despite their widespread\nuse, the comparative performance of these strategies on different\nLLM architectures remains underexplored. To address this gap,\nthis paper investigates the effects of these pooling mechanisms\non two prominent LLM families – BERT and GPT, in the context\nof sentence-level sentiment analysis. Comprehensive experiments\nreveal that each pooling mechanism exhibits unique strengths and\nweaknesses depending on the task’s specific requirements. Our\nfindings underline the importance of selecting pooling methods\ntailored to the demands of particular applications, prompting a\nre-evaluation of common assumptions regarding pooling opera\u0002tions. By offering actionable insights, this study contributes to the\noptimization of LLM-based models for downstream tasks.\nCCS CONCEPTS\n• Computing methodologies → Machine learning; Natural\nlanguage processing; • Information systems → Data mining;\nInformation retrieval.\nKEYWORDS\nLarge Language Models, Pooling Mechanisms, Sentiment Analysis,\nToken Embedding Aggregation\n1 INTRODUCTION\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as transformative\ntools in natural language processing (NLP), offering unparalleled\nperformance across a broad spectrum of tasks. Among these, BERT\n(Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) [1] and\nGPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer) [2] stand out as two of\nthe most influential architectures. BERT’s bidirectional attention\nmechanism enables it to deeply understand contextual relationships\nwithin text, making it particularly effective for comprehension\u0002based tasks. Together, these models exemplify the state\nof the art in leveraging transformer-based architectures to tackle\ndiverse linguistic challenges.\nThe applications of LLMs can be broadly categorized into token\u0002level and sentence-level tasks [3]. Abstract:\n> The present study explores the interpretability of latent spaces produced by time series foundation models, focusing on their potential for visual analysis tasks. Specifically, we evaluate the MOMENT family of models, a set of transformer-based, pre-trained architectures for multivariate time series tasks such as: imputation, prediction, classification, and anomaly detection. We evaluate the capacity of these models on five datasets to capture the underlying structures in time series data within their latent space projection and validate whether fine tuning improves the clarity of the resulting embedding spaces.       "id": "https://arxiv.org/html/2409.12915v1",
      "title": "Unveiling and Manipulating Concepts in Time Series Foundation Models",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2409.12915v1",
      "text": "Unveiling and Manipulating Concepts in Time Series Foundation Models\n\n# Unveiling and Manipulating Concepts in Time Series Foundation Models\n\nMichał Wiliński Mononito Goswami Nina Żukowska Willa Potosnak Artur Dubrawski Auton Lab, School of Computer Science, Carnegie Mellon University Pittsburgh, PA 15213 {mwilinsk,mgoswami}@andrew.cmu.edu\n\n###### Abstract\n\nTime series foundation models promise to be powerful tools for a wide range of applications. However, little is known about the concepts that these models learn and how can we manipulate them in the latent space. Our study bridges these gaps by identifying concepts learned by these models, localizing them to specific parts of the model, and steering model predictions along these conceptual directions, using synthetic time series data. Our results show that MOMENT, a state-of-the-art foundation model, can discern distinct time series patterns, and that this ability peaks in the middle layers of the network. Moreover, we show that model outputs can be steered using insights from its activations (e.g., by introducing periodic trends to initially constant signals through intervention during inference). Our findings underscore the importance of synthetic data in studying and steering time series foundation models and intervening throughout the whole model (using steering matrices), instead of a single layer.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nFoundation models have taken significant strides in modeling both textual (Brown et al., 2020) and visual (Dosovitskiy et al., 2020) data, and have made complex language and image processing accessible to non-experts. These models are pre-trained on massive internet-scale datasets and can be used to solve multiple tasks across a variety of domains, with little to no adaptation. Recently, a growing body of work (Garza and Mergenthaler-Canseco, 2023; Goswami et al., 2024; Rasul et al., 2024; Das et al., 2024; Woo et al., 2024; Ansari et al., 2024) has extended the benefits of this paradigm to time series data, a modality prevalent in fields such as finance (Taylor, 2008), healthcare (Goswami et al., 2021), and climate science (Schneider and Dickinson, 1974).\n\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) have shown promising performance on multiple modeling tasks such as forecasting, classification, anomaly detection, and imputation, across a wide range of domains, and in settings with varying amounts of data and supervision. However, little is known about why these models work, the kinds of concepts that these models are learning, and how can these concepts be manipulated to influence model outputs.\n\nIn this paper, we take initial steps towards addressing these gaps by identifying concepts learned by these models, localizing them to a small subset of their hidden states, and steering model predictions along these conceptual directions, using only synthetic time series. To the best of our knowledge, our work is among the first to identify, localize, and manipulate learned concepts in ",

      "title": "On the Internal Semantics of Time-Series Foundation Models",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2511.15324v1",
      "text": "On the Internal Semantics of Time-Series Foundation Models\n\n# On the Internal Semantics of Time-Series Foundation Models\n\nAtharva Pandey Kairosity atpan@kairosity.ai &Abhilash Neog11footnotemark: 1 Virginia Tech abhilash22@vt.edu &Gautam Jajoo Kairosity jajoo@kairosity.ai Equal contribution.\n\n###### Abstract\n\nTime-series Foundation Models (TSFMs) have recently emerged as a universal paradigm for learning across diverse temporal domains. However, despite their empirical success, the internal mechanisms by which these models represent fundamental time-series concepts remain poorly understood. In this work, we undertake a systematic investigation of concept interpretability in TSFMs. Specifically, we examine: (i) which layers encode which concepts, (ii) whether concept parameters are linearly recoverable, (iii) how representations evolve in terms of concept disentanglement and abstraction across model depth, and (iv) how models process compositions of concepts. We systematically probe these questions using layer-wise analyses, linear recoverability tests, and representation similarity measures, providing a structured account of TSFM semantics. The resulting insights show that early layers mainly capture local, time-domain patterns (e.g., AR(1), level shifts, trends), while deeper layers encode dispersion and change-time signals, with spectral and warping factors remaining the hardest to recover linearly. In compositional settings, however, probe performance degrades, revealing interference between concepts. This highlights that while atomic concepts are reliably localized, composition remains a challenge, underscoring a key limitation in current TSFMs’ ability to represent interacting temporal phenomena.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nFoundation models have recently been extended to time series, where large-scale pretraining over heterogeneous temporal data yields strong zero/few-shot performance in forecasting and classification across healthcare, finance, climate, and energy (timesfm; chronos; moment; moirai; timegpt). Yet, unlike language and vision, our understanding of what these models encode internally remains limited. Interpretability in NLP and CV has shown that probing methods like linear and structural probes as well as representational similarity can localize information across layers and provide insight into model organization (alain2016linear; hewitt2019structural; kornblith2019cka). For TSFMs, early studies such as (wilinski2024tsfm_interventions) reveal block-like layer similarity and the success of latent interventions, underscoring the value of probing. Complementary instance-level explanations in time series, e.g., saliency, attribution, and shapelets, offer rationales for individual predictions but do not illuminate model-wide semantics (ismail2020benchmark; grabocka2014shapelets).\n\nThis gap motivates a systematic investigation into how TSFMs internally represent fundamental time-series phenomena. We study concept interpretability in TSFMs ",

      "id": "https://proceedings.mlr.press/v235/goswami24a.html",
      "title": "MOMENT: A Family of Open Time-series Foundation Models",
      "url": "https://proceedings.mlr.press/v235/goswami24a.html",
      "publishedDate": "2024-07-08T00:00:00.000Z",
      "text": "MOMENT: A Family of Open Time-series Foundation Models\n\n# MOMENT: A Family of Open Time-series Foundation Models\n\nMononito Goswami, Konrad Szafer, Arjun Choudhry, Yifu Cai, Shuo Li, Artur Dubrawski\n\nProceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning, PMLR 235:16115-16152, 2024.\n\n#### Abstract\n\nWe introduce MOMENT, a family of open-source foundation models for general-purpose time series analysis. Pre-training large models on time series data is challenging due to (1) the absence of a large and cohesive public time series repository, and (2) diverse time series characteristics which make multi-dataset training onerous. Additionally, (3) experimental benchmarks to evaluate these models, especially in scenarios with limited resources, time, and supervision, are still in their nascent stages. To address these challenges, we compile a large and diverse collection of public time series, called the Time series Pile, and systematically tackle time series-specific challenges to unlock large-scale multi-dataset pre-training. Finally, we build on recent work to design a benchmark to evaluate time series foundation models on diverse tasks and datasets in limited supervision settings. Experiments on this benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of our pre-trained models with minimal data and task-specific fine-tuning. Finally, we present several interesting empirical observations about large pre-trained time series models. Pre-trained models (AutonLab/MOMENT-1-large) and Time Series Pile (AutonLab/Timeseries-PILE) are available on Huggingface.\n\n#### Cite this Paper\n\n---\n\nBibTeX\n\n`@InProceedings{pmlr-v235-goswami24a,\n title = {{MOMENT}: A Family of Open Time-series Foundation Models},\n author = {Goswami, Mononito and Szafer, Konrad and Choudhry, Arjun and Cai, Yifu and Li, Shuo and Dubrawski, Artur},\n booktitle = {Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning},\n pages = {16115--16152},\n year = {2024},\n editor = {Salakhutdinov, Ruslan and Kolter, Zico and Heller, Katherine and Weller, Adrian and Oliver, Nuria and Scarlett, Jonathan and Berkenkamp, Felix},\n volume = {235},\n series = {Proceedings of Machine Learning Research},\n month = {21--27 Jul},\n publisher = {PMLR},\n pdf = {https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mlresearch/v235/main/assets/goswami24a/goswami24a.pdf},\n url = {https://proceedings.mlr.press/v235/goswami24a.html},\n abstract = {We introduce MOMENT, a family of open-source foundation models for general-purpose time series analysis.       "text": "# MOMENT: A FAMILY OF OPEN TIME-SERIES FOUNDATION MODELS\n\nMononito Goswami1 Konrad Szafer*1 Arjun Choudhry*1 Yifu Cai1 Shuo Li2 Artur Dubrawski1\n\n1Carnegie Mellon University, 2University of Pennsylvania\n\n* Equal contribution\n\n2024\n\n---\n\nWe introduce MOMENT, a family of open-source foundation models for general-purpose time-series analysis. Pre-training large models on time-series data is challenging due to (1) the absence of a large and cohesive public time-series repository, and (2) diverse time-series characteristics which make multi-dataset training onerous. Additionally, (3) experimental benchmarks to evaluate these models, especially in scenarios with limited resources, time, and supervision, are still in their nascent stages. To address these challenges, we compile a large and diverse collection of public time-series, called the Time-series Pile, and systematically tackle time-series-specific challenges to unlock large-scale multi-dataset pre-training. Finally, we build on recent work to design a benchmark to evaluate time-series foundation models on diverse tasks and datasets in limited supervision settings. Experiments on this benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of our pre-trained models with minimal data and task-specific fine-tuning. Finally, we present several interesting empirical observations about large pre-trained time-series models.\n\n---\n\n# The Time-series Pile\n\nWe compiled a large collection of publicly available datasets from diverse domains into the Time Series Pile. | **U2** "canonical pooling families used in sequence representation learning" | **Ennadir et al. 2025** *Pool Me Wisely: On the Effect of Pooling in Transformer-Based Models* (arXiv 2510.03339) — explicitly characterizes the expressivity of widely-used pooling methods on transformers, covering NLP / vision / **time series**. Better match than Sentence-BERT for your "canonical families" claim. |
| **U5** "residual shape heterogeneity (attention patterns, temporal structure) persists in hidden states" | **Wiliński et al. 2025** *Exploring Representations and Interventions in Time Series Foundation Models* (ICML) and **Pandey et al. 2025** *On the Internal Semantics of Time-Series Foundation Models* — both are probing studies of MOMENT/TSFM hidden states showing they encode periodicity, trends, AR(1), level shifts, change points. Pandey's "early layers capture local time-domain patterns" directly substantiates "shape heterogeneity persists." Wiliński is the existing related-work cousin (CMU/Auton lab, MOMENT authors). |

      "publishedDate": "2025-10-02T00:00:00.000Z",
      "text": "[2510.03339] Pool Me Wisely: On the Effect of Pooling in Transformer-Based Models\n\n# Pool Me Wisely: On the Effect of Pooling in Transformer-Based Models\n\nSofiane Ennadir∗ King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming sofiane.ennadir@king.com &Levente Zólyomi∗† NXAI GmbH levente.zolyomi@nx-ai.com &Oleg Smirnov King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming oleg.smirnov@microsoft.com &Tianze Wang† Kreditz AB tianze.wang@kreditz.com &John Pertoft King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming john.pertoft@king.com &Filip Cornell† Amazon filipco@amazon.com &Lele Cao King AI Labs, Microsoft Gaming lelecao@microsoft.com\n\n###### Abstract\n\nTransformer models have become the dominant backbone for sequence modeling, leveraging self-attention to produce contextualized token representations. These are typically aggregated into fixed-size vectors via pooling operations for downstream tasks. While much of the literature has focused on attention mechanisms, the role of pooling remains underexplored despite its critical impact on model behavior. In this paper, we introduce a theoretical framework that rigorously characterizes the expressivity of Transformer-based models equipped with widely used pooling methods by deriving closed-form bounds on their representational capacity and the ability to distinguish similar inputs. Our analysis extends to different variations of attention formulations, demonstrating that these bounds hold across diverse architectural variants. We empirically evaluate pooling strategies across tasks requiring both global and local contextual understanding, spanning three major modalities: computer vision, natural language processing, and time-series analysis. Results reveal consistent trends in how pooling choices affect accuracy, sensitivity, and optimization behavior. Our findings unify theoretical and empirical perspectives, providing practical guidance for selecting or designing pooling mechanisms suited to specific tasks. This work positions pooling as a key architectural component in Transformer models and lays the foundation for more principled model design beyond attention alone.\n\n**footnotetext: Equal contribution.$\\dagger$$\\dagger$footnotetext: Work conducted while at King AI Labs.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nThe profound impact of the Transformer (vaswani2017attention, ) architectures across different modalities and in cross-modal modeling cannot be underestimated. These models have become the building stones of large-scale systems capable of successfully addressing a multitude of downstream tasks in computer vision (awais2025foundation, ), natural language processing (NLP) (touvron2023llama, ; jiang2023mistral7b, ), and time series analysis (liang2024foundation, ; goswami2024moment, ). Since these models typically require substantial training data to perform effectively, pre-trained large-scale models trained with self-supervised objectives such as autoregressive, autoencoding, contrastive, or hybrid formulations have become the standard.       "text": "Exploring Representations and Interventions in Time Series Foundation Models | OpenReview\n\n## Exploring Representations and Interventions in Time Series Foundation Models\n\n### Michał Wiliński, Mononito Goswami, Willa Potosnak, Nina Żukowska, Artur Dubrawski \n\nICML 2025 posterEveryone Revisions BibTeX CC BY-SA 4.0\n\nTL;DR: We investigate why time series foundation models work, the kinds of concepts that these models learn, and how can these concepts be manipulated to influence their outputs?\n\nAbstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) promise to be powerful tools for a wide range of applications. However, their internal representations and learned concepts are still not well understood. In this study, we investigate the structure and redundancy of representations across various TSFMs, examining the self-similarity of model layers within and across different model sizes. This analysis reveals block-like redundancy in the representations, which can be utilized for informed pruning to improve inference speed and efficiency. We also explore the concepts learned by these models, such as periodicity and trends. We demonstrate how conceptual priors can be derived from TSFM representations and leveraged to steer its outputs toward concept-informed predictions. Our work bridges representational analysis from language and vision models to TSFMs, offering new methods for building more computationally efficient and transparent TSFMs.\n\nLay Summary: Everyday things like the weather, stock prices, heart-rate readings or traffic counts are all examples of time-series data, i.e. numbers that arrive one after another over time. Researchers have recently begun training very large, general-purpose AI models on many kinds of these sequences. Because a single such model can “found” (i.e., serve as the starting point for) many different forecasting tasks, scientists call them foundation models — similar to how ChatGPT is a single text model used for lots of language tasks. In this study we opened that black box. First, we peered inside several leading time-series foundation models and found that many of their layers were doing almost identical work. By safely trimming these duplicates we cut the models’ size and made their predictions up to 50% faster, without harming accuracy. Next, we asked what the models actually know. We discovered they naturally learn simple concepts people care about, such as whether a signal is flat, rising, falling or repeating in cycles. Finally, we showed that we can gently turn the model’s internal “knobs” to inject these concepts on demand: for example, turning a flat forecast into one that rises, or adding a seasonal ups-and-downs pattern. Crucially, this steering happens after training, so users don’t need to retrain the model or supply extra data. Together, these findings make large time-series AI models cheaper to run, easier to understand and easier to control, paving the way for safer, more efficient tools in areas from finance to",

Our work bridges representational analysis from language and vision models to TSFMs, offering new methods for building more computationally efficient and transparent TSFMs.\n\nShow more\n\n### Lay Summary\n\nEveryday things like the weather, stock prices, heart-rate readings or traffic counts are all examples of time-series data, i.e. numbers that arrive one after another over time. Researchers have recently begun training very large, general-purpose AI models on many kinds of these sequences. Because a single such model can “found” (i.e., serve as the starting point for) many different forecasting tasks, scientists call them foundation models — similar to how ChatGPT is a single text model used for lots of language tasks.In this study we opened that black box. We discovered they naturally learn simple concepts people care about, such as whether a signal is flat, rising, falling or repeating in cycles. Finally, we showed that we can gently turn the model’s internal “knobs” to inject these concepts on demand: for example, turning a flat forecast into one that rises, or adding a seasonal ups-and-downs pattern. Crucially, this steering happens after training, so users don’t need to retrain the model or supply extra data.Together, these findings make large time-series AI models cheaper to run, easier to understand and easier to control, paving the way for safer, more efficient tools in areas from finance to healthcare.\n\nShow more\n\n### Video\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load",

      "text": "On the Internal Semantics of Time-Series Foundation Models | OpenReview\n\n## On the Internal Semantics of Time-Series Foundation Models\n\n### Atharva Pandey, Abhilash Neog, Gautam Jajoo\n\nBERT2Severyonesince 09 Oct 2025\"Everyone Revisions BibTeX CC BY 4.0\n\nKeywords: Time-series foundation models, Representation learning, interpretability\n\nTL;DR: Probing TSFMs to find internal representation of time series concepts\n\nAbstract: Time-series Foundation Models (TSFMs) have recently emerged as a universal paradigm for learning across diverse temporal domains. However, despite their empirical success, the internal mechanisms by which these models represent fundamental time-series concepts remain poorly understood. In this work, we undertake a systematic investigation of concept interpretability in TSFMs. Specifically, we examine: (i) which layers encode which concepts, (ii) whether concept parameters are linearly recoverable, (iii) how representations evolve in terms of concept disentanglement and abstraction across model depth, and (iv) how models process compositions of concepts. We systematically probe these questions using layer-wise analyses, linear recoverability tests, and representation similarity measures, providing a structured account of TSFM semantics. The resulting insights show that early layers mainly capture local, time-domain patterns (e.g., AR(1), level shifts, trends), while deeper layers encode dispersion and change-time signals, with spectral and warping factors remaining the hardest to recover linearly. In compositional settings, however, probe performance degrades, revealing interference between concepts. This highlights that while atomic concepts are reliably localized, composition remains a challenge, underscoring a key limitation in current TSFMs' ability to represent interacting temporal phenomena.\n\nSubmission Number: 35\n\nLoading",

  "old_string": "Standard TSFM adapters~\\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora} pool hidden states and project them to the forecast horizon through the same head for every window (Figure~\\ref{fig:problem_overview}a). Real time series mix heterogeneous regimes (quiet baselines, seasonal excursions, abrupt shape changes), and instance normalization handles only the scale-and-offset component of that heterogeneity; residual \\emph{shape} heterogeneity (attention patterns, temporal structure) persists in the hidden states and is what motivates a per-window mixture of expert adapters: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one.",
  "new_string": "Standard TSFM adapters~\\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora} pool hidden states and project them to the forecast horizon through the same head for every window (Figure~\\ref{fig:problem_overview}a). Real time series mix heterogeneous regimes (quiet baselines, seasonal excursions, abrupt shape changes), and instance normalization handles only the scale-and-offset component of that heterogeneity; residual \\emph{shape} heterogeneity (periodicity, trends, level shifts, change points) is encoded in the hidden states across layers, as recent probing analyses of MOMENT and related TSFMs have shown~\\citep{wilinski2025tsfm_representations, pandey2025tsfm_semantics}, motivating a per-window mixture of expert adapters: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one."
}

662:MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization needed for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies where it applies. The broader pattern: when a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, the fix is restoring the input, not retuning the optimizer (replicated in a vision control, Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). \textbf{Limits:} \emph{(i)}~instance-level normalization only (attention-based/learned normalizers uncharacterized); \emph{(ii)}~$R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy, not sharp on shape-statistics-coupled data (Solar, $R{=}0.06$); \emph{(iii)}~forecasting and imputation only.\label{page:end_main}
```
